=== SOURCE CODE (cpp) ===

#include <algorithm> 
#include <sstream>   

#include <string>

class NumberWordFormatter {
public:
    NumberWordFormatter();

    std::string format(int x);
    std::string format(double x);
    std::string format(const std::string& x);
    std::string format(std::nullptr_t);
    std::string trans_two(const std::string& s);
    std::string trans_three(const std::string& s);
    std::string parse_more(int i);

private:
    const std::string NUMBER[10];
    const std::string NUMBER_TEEN[10];
    const std::string NUMBER_TEN[9];
    const std::string NUMBER_MORE[4];
    const std::string NUMBER_SUFFIX[20];
};


NumberWordFormatter::NumberWordFormatter()
    : NUMBER{ "", "ONE", "TWO", "THREE", "FOUR", "FIVE", "SIX", "SEVEN", "EIGHT", "NINE" },
    NUMBER_TEEN{ "TEN", "ELEVEN", "TWELVE", "THIRTEEN", "FOURTEEN", "FIFTEEN", "SIXTEEN", "SEVENTEEN", "EIGHTEEN", "NINETEEN" },
    NUMBER_TEN{ "TEN", "TWENTY", "THIRTY", "FORTY", "FIFTY", "SIXTY", "SEVENTY", "EIGHTY", "NINETY" },
    NUMBER_MORE{ "", "THOUSAND", "MILLION", "BILLION" },
    NUMBER_SUFFIX{ "k", "w", "", "m", "", "", "b", "", "", "t", "", "", "p", "", "", "e" } {}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::format(int x) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << x;
    return format(ss.str());
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::format(double x) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << x;
    return format(ss.str());
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::format(const std::string& x) {
    std::string lstr = x.substr(0, x.find('.'));
    std::string rstr;
    auto now = x.find('.');
    if (x.find('.') != std::string::npos)
    {
        rstr = x.substr(x.find('.') + 1);
    }
    std::reverse(lstr.begin(), lstr.end());

    if (lstr.size() % 3 == 1) lstr += "00";
    else if (lstr.size() % 3 == 2) lstr += "0";

    std::string a[5] = { "", "", "", "", "" };
    std::string lm = "";

    for (size_t i = 0; i < lstr.size() / 3; ++i) {
        a[i] = std::string(1, lstr[3 * i + 2]) + std::string(1, lstr[3 * i + 1]) + std::string(1, lstr[3 * i]);
        
        if (a[i] != "000") {
            lm = trans_three(a[i]) + " " + parse_more(i) + " " + lm;
        }
        else {
            lm += trans_three(a[i]);
        }
    }
    std::string xs = !rstr.empty() ? "AND CENTS " + trans_two(rstr) + " " : "";
    if (lm.empty()) {
        return "ZERO ONLY";
    }
    else {
        int nowpos = 0;
        for (int i = lm.size() - 1; i >= 0; --i)
        {
            if (lm[i] != ' ')
            {
                nowpos = i;
                break;
            }
        }
        lm = lm.substr(0, nowpos + 1);
        return lm + " " + xs + "ONLY";
    }
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::format(std::nullptr_t) {
    return "";
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::trans_two(const std::string& s) {
    std::string ss = s;
    if (ss.size() == 1) ss = "0" + ss;

    if (ss[0] == '0') {
        return NUMBER[ss[1] - '0'];
    }
    else if (ss[0] == '1') {
        return NUMBER_TEEN[std::stoi(ss) - 10];
    }
    else if (ss[1] == '0') {
        return NUMBER_TEN[ss[0] - '1'];
    }
    else {
        return NUMBER_TEN[ss[0] - '1'] + " " + NUMBER[ss[1] - '0'];
    }
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::trans_three(const std::string& s) {
    if (s[0] == '0') {
        return trans_two(s.substr(1));
    }
    else if (s.substr(1) == "00") {
        return NUMBER[s[0] - '0'] + " HUNDRED";
    }
    else {
        return NUMBER[s[0] - '0'] + " HUNDRED AND " + trans_two(s.substr(1));
    }
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::parse_more(int i) {
    return NUMBER_MORE[i];
}

```cpp
#include <algorithm> 
#include <sstream>   

#include <string>

class NumberWordFormatter {
public:
    NumberWordFormatter();

    std::string format(int x);
    std::string format(double x);
    std::string format(const std::string& x);
    std::string format(std::nullptr_t);
    std::string trans_two(const std::string& s);
    std::string trans_three(const std::string& s);
    std::string parse_more(int i);

private:
    const std::string NUMBER[10];
    const std::string NUMBER_TEEN[10];
    const std::string NUMBER_TEN[9];
    const std::string NUMBER_MORE[4];
    const std::string NUMBER_SUFFIX[20];
};


NumberWordFormatter::NumberWordFormatter()
    : NUMBER{ "", "ONE", "TWO", "THREE", "FOUR", "FIVE", "SIX", "SEVEN", "EIGHT", "NINE" },
    NUMBER_TEEN{ "TEN", "ELEVEN", "TWELVE", "THIRTEEN", "FOURTEEN", "FIFTEEN", "SIXTEEN", "SEVENTEEN", "EIGHTEEN", "NINETEEN" },
    NUMBER_TEN{ "TEN", "TWENTY", "THIRTY", "FORTY", "FIFTY", "SIXTY", "SEVENTY", "EIGHTY", "NINETY" },
    NUMBER_MORE{ "", "THOUSAND", "MILLION", "BILLION" },
    NUMBER_SUFFIX{ "k", "w", "", "m", "", "", "b", "", "", "t", "", "", "p", "", "", "e" } {}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::format(int x) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << x;
    return format(ss.str());
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::format(double x) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << x;
    return format(ss.str());
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::format(const std::string& x) {
    std::string lstr = x.substr(0, x.find('.'));
    std::string rstr;
    auto now = x.find('.');
    if (x.find('.') != std::string::npos)
    {
        rstr = x.substr(x.find('.') + 1);
    }
    std::reverse(lstr.begin(), lstr.end());

    if (lstr.size() % 3 == 1) lstr += "00";
    else if (lstr.size() % 3 == 2) lstr += "0";

    std::string a[5] = { "", "", "", "", "" };
    std::string lm = "";

    for (size_t i = 0; i < lstr.size() / 3; ++i) {
        a[i] = std::string(1, lstr[3 * i + 2]) + std::string(1, lstr[3 * i + 1]) + std::string(1, lstr[3 * i]);
        
        if (a[i] != "000") {
            lm = trans_three(a[i]) + " " + parse_more(i) + " " + lm;
        }
        else {
            lm += trans_three(a[i]);
        }
    }
    std::string xs = !rstr.empty() ? "AND CENTS " + trans_two(rstr) + " " : "";
    if (lm.empty()) {
        return "ZERO ONLY";
    }
    else {
        int nowpos = 0;
        for (int i = lm.size() - 1; i >= 0; --i)
        {
            if (lm[i] != ' ')
            {
                nowpos = i;
                break;
            }
        }
        lm = lm.substr(0, nowpos + 1);
        return lm + " " + xs + "ONLY";
    }
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::format(std::nullptr_t) {
    return "";
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::trans_two(const std::string& s) {
    std::string ss = s;
    if (ss.size() == 1) ss = "0" + ss;

    if (ss[0] == '0') {
        return NUMBER[ss[1] - '0'];
    }
    else if (ss[0] == '1') {
        return NUMBER_TEEN[std::stoi(ss) - 10];
    }
    else if (ss[1] == '0') {
        return NUMBER_TEN[ss[0] - '1'];
    }
    else {
        return NUMBER_TEN[ss[0] - '1'] + " " + NUMBER[ss[1] - '0'];
    }
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::trans_three(const std::string& s) {
    if (s[0] == '0') {
        return trans_two(s.substr(1));
    }
    else if (s.substr(1) == "00") {
        return NUMBER[s[0] - '0'] + " HUNDRED";
    }
    else {
        return NUMBER[s[0] - '0'] + " HUNDRED AND " + trans_two(s.substr(1));
    }
}

std::string NumberWordFormatter::parse_more(int i) {
    return NUMBER_MORE[i];
}
```

Now, the string format algorithm.

Let's write Python code.

Pseudo:

class NumberWordFormatter:
    NUMBER = ["", "ONE", ...]
    NUMBER_TEEN = ["TEN", ...]
    NUMBER_TEN = ["TEN", ...]
    NUMBER_MORE = ["", "THOUSAND", "MILLION", "BILLION"]
    NUMBER_SUFFIX = ["k", "w", "", "m", "", "", "b", "", "", "t", "", "", "p", "", "", "e"]  # maybe length 20? In C++ it's 20 with remaining empty. We'll pad to 20.

    def __init__(self): pass? Not needed.

    def format(self, x):
        if x is None: return ""
        if isinstance(x, bool): # C++ bool to int? But bool is int. We'll treat as int.
            return self.format(str(int(x)))  # Actually format(int) uses stringstream << int. For bool, C++ would output "1" or "0"? Let's check: bool is integral type, ostream has overload for bool? Actually std::ostream has operator<<(bool) which outputs "1" or "0" (unless boolalpha). But overload resolution: format(bool) could convert to int? There is no format(bool), but bool can convert to int exactly, so format(int) chosen. So output "1" or "0". In Python, bool is int, so str(True) is "True", not "1". Need to handle bool separately: if isinstance(x, bool): x = int(x). Then proceed.
        if isinstance(x, int):
            return self.format(str(x))
        if isinstance(x, float):
            return self.format(self._double_to_string(x))
        if isinstance(x, str):
            return self._format_string(x)
        # else maybe raise TypeError? C++ no equivalent. We'll just let.

But wait: C++ has format(const std::string& x) and format(std::nullptr_t). There is no format(const char*) but string can be constructed from const char*. In Python, str covers. Good.

Need to implement `_double_to_string` using format(x, '.6g')? But there is a nuance: Python's format with '.6g' for 0.0 returns '0'. C++ stringstream << 0.0 returns "0". Good. For -0.0, Python format(-0.0, '.6g') returns '-0'. C++? Let's test mentally: printf("%g", -0.0) -> "-0". Yes.

But wait: Python's format(x, '.6g') for a float like 1e-6 returns '1e-06'? Actually Python's exponent format uses at least two digits? Let's check: format(1e-6, '.6g') -> '1e-06'. C++ printf("%g", 1e-6) -> "1e-06". Good. For 1e20, Python -> '1e+20'. C++ -> "1e+20". Good.

But there is a difference: Python's g may output "1e+20" but C++ may output "1e+20" (with plus). Yes.

However, C++ stringstream uses the current locale's decimal point, but default "C" uses ".". Python uses ".".

So use `format(x, '.6g')`.

But wait: C++ default precision is 6, but for `double` values with more than 6 significant digits, it rounds. Python `.6g` also rounds. Good.

Now, `_format_string(self, x)`:

    lstr = x.split('.')[0]? But C++ uses find('.') and substr. If x contains no '.', rstr empty. If x contains multiple '.', C++ uses first '.' for lstr, and rstr is substring after first '.' (including any further dots). In Python, `x.find('.')` works. We'll implement similarly.

    dot = x.find('.')
    if dot == -1:
        lstr = x
        rstr = ''
    else:
        lstr = x[:dot]
        rstr = x[dot+1:]

    lstr = lstr[::-1]  # reverse

    if len(lstr) % 3 == 1:
        lstr += "00"
    elif len(lstr) % 3 == 2:
        lstr += "0"

    a = ["", "", "", "", ""]  # size 5
    lm = ""

    for i in range(len(lstr) // 3):
        a[i] = lstr[3*i+2] + lstr[3*i+1] + lstr[3*i]
        if a[i] != "000":
            lm = self.trans_three(a[i]) + " " + self.parse_more(i) + " " + lm
        else:
            lm += self.trans_three(a[i])

    xs = ""
    if rstr:
        xs = "AND CENTS " + self.trans_two(rstr) + " "

    if lm == "":
        return "ZERO ONLY"
    else:
        # trim trailing spaces
        lm = lm.rstrip()  # C++ removes trailing spaces by finding last non-space. rstrip removes all trailing whitespace, but lm only has spaces. Good.
        return lm + " " + xs + "ONLY"

Wait: C++ trims trailing spaces from lm. But note if lm has leading spaces? It builds lm by prepending, so no leading spaces. rstrip is fine.

But careful: In C++, when a[i] == "000", lm += trans_three(a[i]) which is "". So no change. So lm remains maybe empty. Good.

Now, trans_two:

    def trans_two(self, s):
        ss = s
        if len(ss) == 1:
            ss = "0" + ss
        if ss[0] == '0':
            return self.NUMBER[int(ss[1])]
        elif ss[0] == '1':
            return self.NUMBER_TEEN[int(ss) - 10]
        elif ss[1] == '0':
            return self.NUMBER_TEN[int(ss[0]) - 1]
        else:
            return self.NUMBER_TEN[int(ss[0]) - 1] + " " + self.NUMBER[int(ss[1])]

But note: `int(ss)` works for "10" etc. For ss like "0-" would error, but ignore.

trans_three:

    def trans_three(self, s):
        if s[0] == '0':
            return self.trans_two(s[1:])
        elif s[1:] == "00":
            return self.NUMBER[int(s[0])] + " HUNDRED"
        else:
            return self.NUMBER[int(s[0])] + " HUNDRED AND " + self.trans_two(s[1:])

parse_more:

    def parse_more(self, i):
        return self.NUMBER_MORE[i]

Need to include NUMBER_SUFFIX even if unused? The C++ class has it as private. It's not used. But to be faithful, include it. It's a list of 20 strings. In C++, initializer list has 16, rest default "". We'll create list with 20 elements, filling missing with "".

Now, potential issue: The C++ `format(const std::string& x)` uses `std::string a[5] = { "", "", "", "", "" };`. If lstr.size()/3 > 5, it writes out of bounds. In Python, if we use list of 5 and loop range(len(lstr)//3), we'll get IndexError if >5. To preserve C++ behavior for valid inputs, we can limit to 5? But C++ would overflow, so not valid. We can use dynamic list to be more robust, but then parse_more(i) for i>=4 would IndexError. Actually C++ NUMBER_MORE[4] is out-of-bounds for i>=4. So valid inputs are up to 12 digits (4 groups). For 15 digits, i=4 would access NUMBER_MORE[4] out-of-bounds. So not valid. We'll keep list of 5 for a, and loop over min(len(lstr)//3, 5)? But C++ would loop over all groups and overflow. To be identical for valid, we can loop over all groups but if >5, Python IndexError. That's acceptable? The problem says keep behavior identical, including edge cases. But undefined behavior is not a defined edge case. We can ignore. To be safe, we can use a list of length len(lstr)//3 and rely on parse_more to fail for i>=4. But then a[i] works for any i. The C++ a[5] is only a buffer; if lstr.size()/3 > 5, it's UB. In Python, using dynamic list would not fail until parse_more. But parse_more would fail for i>=4. Actually C++ parse_more would also fail (UB). So dynamic list is fine. But to mimic the buffer size? Not necessary.

However, there is another subtlety: In C++, `std::string a[5]` is uninitialized? It's initialized with empty strings. If lstr.size()/3 < 5, remaining a entries remain "". But they are not used. In Python, we can just use a list of that size.

Let's implement with `a = [""] * 5` to match. Then loop `for i in range(len(lstr) // 3):` and assign a[i]. If len(lstr)//3 > 5, IndexError. But that's UB in C++. We'll leave.

But wait: For normal int, max 10 digits, after padding len(lstr)//3 = 4 (since 10 digits -> reverse, pad to 12 -> 4 groups). So within 5. Good.

Then it accesses ss[0], ss[1], and in `else if (ss[1] == '0')` etc. It never uses ss[2] or beyond. In the final else, it uses ss[0] and ss[1]. So indeed it ignores characters after the first two. Now, trimming: C++:
```
int nowpos = 0;
for (int i = lm.size() - 1; i >= 0; --i) {
    if (lm[i] != ' ') { nowpos = i; break; }
}
lm = lm.substr(0, nowpos + 1);
```
This removes trailing spaces. If lm is all spaces? But lm is never all spaces; if non-empty, it has at least one non-space. If lm empty, handled earlier. Python `lm.rstrip()` removes all trailing whitespace, including tabs/newlines, but lm only contains spaces. Equivalent. Let's ensure code is syntactically correct.

```python
class NumberWordFormatter:
    NUMBER = ["", "ONE", "TWO", "THREE", "FOUR", "FIVE", "SIX", "SEVEN", "EIGHT", "NINE"]
    NUMBER_TEEN = ["TEN", "ELEVEN", "TWELVE", "THIRTEEN", "FOURTEEN", "FIFTEEN", "SIXTEEN", "SEVENTEEN", "EIGHTEEN", "NINETEEN"]
    NUMBER_TEN = ["TEN", "TWENTY", "THIRTY", "FORTY", "FIFTY", "SIXTY", "SEVENTY", "EIGHTY", "NINETY"]
    NUMBER_MORE = ["", "THOUSAND", "MILLION", "BILLION"]
    NUMBER_SUFFIX = ["k", "w", "", "m", "", "", "b", "", "", "t", "", "", "p", "", "", "e", "", "", "", "", ""]

    def __init__(self):
        pass

    def format(self, x):
        if x is None:
            return ""
        if isinstance(x, bool):
            return self.format(str(int(x)))
        if isinstance(x, int):
            return self.format(str(x))
        if isinstance(x, float):
            return self.format(format(x, '.6g'))
        if isinstance(x, str):
            return self._format_string(x)
        # For other types, mimic C++? There is no overload. Raise TypeError.
        raise TypeError("Unsupported type")

    def _format_string(self, x):
        dot = x.find('.')
        if dot == -1:
            lstr = x
            rstr = ""
        else:
            lstr = x[:dot]
            rstr = x[dot+1:]

        lstr = lstr[::-1]

        if len(lstr) % 3 == 1:
            lstr += "00"
        elif len(lstr) % 3 == 2:
            lstr += "0"

        a = [""] * 5
        lm = ""

        for i in range(len(lstr) // 3):
            a[i] = lstr[3*i+2] + lstr[3*i+1] + lstr[3*i]
            if a[i] != "000":
                lm = self.trans_three(a[i]) + " " + self.parse_more(i) + " " + lm
            else:
                lm += self.trans_three(a[i])

        xs = ""
        if rstr:
            xs = "AND CENTS " + self.trans_two(rstr) + " "

        if lm == "":
            return "ZERO ONLY"
        else:
            lm = lm.rstrip(' ')
            return lm + " " + xs + "ONLY"

    def trans_two(self, s):
        ss = s
        if len(ss) == 1:
            ss = "0" + ss
        if ss[0] == '0':
            return self.NUMBER[int(ss[1])]
        elif ss[0] == '1':
            return self.NUMBER_TEEN[int(ss) - 10]
        elif ss[1] == '0':
            return self.NUMBER_TEN[int(ss[0]) - 1]
        else:
            return self.NUMBER_TEN[int(ss[0]) - 1] + " " + self.NUMBER[int(ss[1])]

    def trans_three(self, s):
        if s[0] == '0':
            return self.trans_two(s[1:])
        elif s[1:] == "00":
            return self.NUMBER[int(s[0])] + " HUNDRED"
        else:
            return self.NUMBER[int(s[0])] + " HUNDRED AND " + self.trans_two(s[1:])

    def parse_more(self, i):
        return self.NUMBER_MORE[i]
```

Wait, in `format` for bool, we used `self.format(str(int(x)))`. But `str(int(True))` -> "1". Good. But we could also just `return self.format(int(x))` which would then call `self.format(str(x))`? Actually `self.format(int(x))` would go to int branch and call `self.format(str(x))` where x is int 1, str(1)="1". So `self.format(str(int(x)))` is fine.

But note: In Python, `isinstance(True, int)` is True, so bool check must come before int. We did.

Now, what about `format` method name: It's fine.

```
nf.format("123.45") -> "ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY THREE AND CENTS FORTY FIVE ONLY"
nf.format(None) -> ""
```

Now, let's think about `format(double)` for 1234567.0. Python format(1234567.0, '.6g') -> '1.23457e+06'. Then _format_string:

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
class NumberWordFormatter:
    NUMBER = ["", "ONE", "TWO", "THREE", "FOUR", "FIVE", "SIX", "SEVEN", "EIGHT", "NINE"]
    NUMBER_TEEN = ["TEN", "ELEVEN", "TWELVE", "THIRTEEN", "FOURTEEN", "FIFTEEN", "SIXTEEN", "SEVENTEEN", "EIGHTEEN", "NINETEEN"]
    NUMBER_TEN = ["TEN", "TWENTY", "THIRTY", "FORTY", "FIFTY", "SIXTY", "SEVENTY", "EIGHTY", "NINETY"]
    NUMBER_MORE = ["", "THOUSAND", "MILLION", "BILLION"]
    NUMBER_SUFFIX = ["k", "w", "", "m", "", "", "b", "", "", "t", "", "", "p", "", "", "e", "", "", "", "", ""]

    def __init__(self):
        pass

    def format(self, x):
        if x is None:
            return ""
        if isinstance(x, bool):
            return self.format(str(int(x)))
        if isinstance(x, int):
            return self.format(str(x))
        if isinstance(x, float):
            return self.format('{:.6g}'.format(x))
        if isinstance(x, str):
            return self._format_string(x)
        raise TypeError("Unsupported type")

    def _format_string(self, x):
        dot = x.find('.')
        if dot == -1:
            lstr = x
            rstr = ""
        else:
            lstr = x[:dot]
            rstr = x[dot + 1:]

        lstr = lstr[::-1]

        if len(lstr) % 3 == 1:
            lstr += "00"
        elif len(lstr) % 3 == 2:
            lstr += "0"

        a = [""] * 5
        lm = ""

        for i in range(len(lstr) // 3):
            a[i] = lstr[3 * i + 2] + lstr[3 * i + 1] + lstr[3 * i]
            if a[i] != "000":
                lm = self.trans_three(a[i]) + " " + self.parse_more(i) + " " + lm
            else:
                lm += self.trans_three(a[i])

        xs = ""
        if rstr:
            xs = "AND CENTS " + self.trans_two(rstr) + " "

        if lm == "":
            return "ZERO ONLY"
        else:
            lm = lm.rstrip(' ')
            return lm + " " + xs + "ONLY"

    def trans_two(self, s):
        ss = s
        if len(ss) == 1:
            ss = "0" + ss
        if ss[0] == '0':
            return self.NUMBER[int(ss[1])]
        elif ss[0] == '1':
            return self.NUMBER_TEEN[int(ss) - 10]
        elif ss[1] == '0':
            return self.NUMBER_TEN[int(ss[0]) - 1]
        else:
            return self.NUMBER_TEN[int(ss[0]) - 1] + " " + self.NUMBER[int(ss[1])]

    def trans_three(self, s):
        if s[0] == '0':
            return self.trans_two(s[1:])
        elif s[1:] == "00":
            return self.NUMBER[int(s[0])] + " HUNDRED"
        else:
            return self.NUMBER[int(s[0])] + " HUNDRED AND " + self.trans_two(s[1:])

    def parse_more(self, i):
        return self.NUMBER_MORE[i]
```

=== TRANSLATED CODE (py) ===
class NumberWordFormatter:
    NUMBER = ["", "ONE", "TWO", "THREE", "FOUR", "FIVE", "SIX", "SEVEN", "EIGHT", "NINE"]
    NUMBER_TEEN = ["TEN", "ELEVEN", "TWELVE", "THIRTEEN", "FOURTEEN", "FIFTEEN", "SIXTEEN", "SEVENTEEN", "EIGHTEEN", "NINETEEN"]
    NUMBER_TEN = ["TEN", "TWENTY", "THIRTY", "FORTY", "FIFTY", "SIXTY", "SEVENTY", "EIGHTY", "NINETY"]
    NUMBER_MORE = ["", "THOUSAND", "MILLION", "BILLION"]
    NUMBER_SUFFIX = ["k", "w", "", "m", "", "", "b", "", "", "t", "", "", "p", "", "", "e", "", "", "", "", ""]

    def __init__(self):
        pass

    def format(self, x):
        if x is None:
            return ""
        if isinstance(x, bool):
            return self.format(str(int(x)))
        if isinstance(x, int):
            return self.format(str(x))
        if isinstance(x, float):
            return self.format('{:.6g}'.format(x))
        if isinstance(x, str):
            return self._format_string(x)
        raise TypeError("Unsupported type")

    def _format_string(self, x):
        dot = x.find('.')
        if dot == -1:
            lstr = x
            rstr = ""
        else:
            lstr = x[:dot]
            rstr = x[dot + 1:]

        lstr = lstr[::-1]

        if len(lstr) % 3 == 1:
            lstr += "00"
        elif len(lstr) % 3 == 2:
            lstr += "0"

        a = [""] * 5
        lm = ""

        for i in range(len(lstr) // 3):
            a[i] = lstr[3 * i + 2] + lstr[3 * i + 1] + lstr[3 * i]
            if a[i] != "000":
                lm = self.trans_three(a[i]) + " " + self.parse_more(i) + " " + lm
            else:
                lm += self.trans_three(a[i])

        xs = ""
        if rstr:
            xs = "AND CENTS " + self.trans_two(rstr) + " "

        if lm == "":
            return "ZERO ONLY"
        else:
            lm = lm.rstrip(' ')
            return lm + " " + xs + "ONLY"

    def trans_two(self, s):
        ss = s
        if len(ss) == 1:
            ss = "0" + ss
        if ss[0] == '0':
            return self.NUMBER[int(ss[1])]
        elif ss[0] == '1':
            return self.NUMBER_TEEN[int(ss) - 10]
        elif ss[1] == '0':
            return self.NUMBER_TEN[int(ss[0]) - 1]
        else:
            return self.NUMBER_TEN[int(ss[0]) - 1] + " " + self.NUMBER[int(ss[1])]

    def trans_three(self, s):
        if s[0] == '0':
            return self.trans_two(s[1:])
        elif s[1:] == "00":
            return self.NUMBER[int(s[0])] + " HUNDRED"
        else:
            return self.NUMBER[int(s[0])] + " HUNDRED AND " + self.trans_two(s[1:])

    def parse_more(self, i):
        return self.NUMBER_MORE[i]
